<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I need to select the most relevant research area keywords for the paper "Reproducibility Budgets for Open-Weight LLM Evaluation: A Generalizability Theory Study of 7–9B Instruction-Tuned Models."

The paper is about:
1. G-theory (psychometric framework) for LLM evaluation variance decomposition
2. Reproducibility/reliability of benchmark evaluations
3. Item analysis, variance components
4. D-study budget projections
5. Cross-benchmark comparison (MMLU, ARC, HellaSwag, GSM8K, MATH)


6. Multiple evaluation facets: prompt, seed, precision, ordering, model, temperature

The most relevant ARR areas are Resources and Evaluation as the primary focus—this is fundamentally about evaluation methodology, benchmarking, reproducibility, and statistical testing for evaluation. Interpretability and Analysis of Models for NLP is also relevant since the paper analyzes model behavior through variance decomposition and examines item-level difficulty differences across models.

For an ARR submission, I'd select Resources and Evaluation as the primary area with either Interpretability and Analysis or Machine Learning for NLP as secondary, depending on whether I want to emphasize the behavioral analysis or the statistical methodology aspects.
```

</details>

{
  "text": "从 https://aclrollingreview.org/areas 中选择的推荐 research area keywords：\n\n---\n\n### Primary Area（强烈推荐）\n\n**Resources and Evaluation**\n- `benchmarking`\n- `evaluation methodologies`\n- `evaluation`\n- `reproducibility`\n- `statistical testing for evaluation`\n- `metrics`\n\n理由：论文核心贡献是 G-theory 方法论用于 LLM 评测的方差分解和可复现性预算，直接落在 evaluation methodology + reproducibility + benchmarking。\n\n---\n\n### Secondary Area（二选一）\n\n**选项 A：Interpretability and Analysis of Models for NLP**（推荐）\n- `robustness`\n- `hardness of samples`\n- `calibration/uncertainty`\n\n理由：论文分析了模型在 item 层面的差异行为（model×item interaction = DIF），本质上是 analysis of model behavior；\"hardness of samples\" 直接对应 item difficulty 作为最大方差成分。\n\n**选项 B：Machine Learning for NLP**\n- `generalization`\n\n理由：论文研究 LLM 评测结果在不同 methodological conditions 下的可泛化性（generalizability），但这个 area 偏方法创新，论文偏分析，匹配度略低于选项 A。\n\n---\n\n### 建议提交组合\n\n| 字段 | 选择 |\n|------|------|\n| Primary Area | **Resources and Evaluation** |\n| Secondary Area | **Interpretability and Analysis of Models for NLP** |\n| Keywords | benchmarking, evaluation methodologies, reproducibility, statistical testing for evaluation, robustness, hardness of samples |\n\n这个组合最准确：主投 evaluation methodology 审稿池（reviewer 懂 psychometrics / benchmarking），副投 model analysis 审稿池（reviewer 懂 robustness / item-level analysis）。"
}

For A2 about risks, the paper doesn't propose new models or involve human subjects, so the risks are minimal—maybe around gaming benchmarks, but that's indirect. For B4 about demographic information, it's clearly not applicable since the work only evaluates existing benchmarks on LLMs without any human participants involved.

The real question is whether to mark something "No" (the question applies but we didn't address it) versus "N/A" (the question doesn't apply to this work). The findings could theoretically be misused to cherry-pick evaluation conditions, but that's a stretch for a methodology paper focused on reproducibility.

For B4, the paper uses established public benchmarks like MMLU and ARC rather than collecting new data, so this is either "No" (didn't need to check because they're well-vetted) or "N/A" (not applicable since no new data collection occurred). I'll go with N/A since the benchmarks themselves have already been scrutinized. The distinction between "No" and "N/A" matters here—A2 and B4 likely apply to the paper since it uses artifacts, but the specific risks don't materialize. For A2, there are genuinely no potential harms to discuss since this is methodological work. For B4, the established benchmarks used are publicly vetted and the paper doesn't introduce new data, so checking for PII/offensive content isn't really applicable. The key principle is recognizing when a section applies to the work but the particular concern doesn't warrant a substantive response.           " \\label{sec:exp002}",
          " ",
          "-Exp-002 extends to five benchmarks: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag (MC), GSM8K, and MATH (FF). The design crosses 8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items, totaling 2,304,000 records. This reduced crossing fixes precision at BF16 to concentrate power on format differences. ```text
175	\section{Model and Prompt Details}
176	\label{app:models}
177	
178	\paragraph{Models.} See Table~\ref{tab:model_ids} (main text) for the eight models. Full HuggingFace identifiers: \texttt{meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct}, \texttt{google/gemma-2-9b-it}, \texttt{mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3}, \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, \texttt{01-ai/Yi-1.5-9B-Chat}, \texttt{allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct}, \texttt{internlm/internlm2\_5-7b-chat}, \texttt{deepseek-ai/deepseek-llm-7b-chat}.
179	
180	\paragraph{Prompt templates.} Our six prompt templates follow a surface perturbation design inspired by BrittleBench \cite{brittlebench2026}. For multiple-choice benchmarks, templates vary along three dimensions: option layout (lettered vs.\ numbered), instruction wording (direct vs.\ contextual), and answer prompt cue. For free-form benchmarks, templates vary chain-of-thought elicitation strategy. All six templates are semantically equivalent: they request the same task with different surface forms, to isolate formatting effects from content effects. ARC-Challenge and HellaSwag use the same six surface patterns as MMLU with domain-appropriate system instructions; MATH mirrors the GSM8K pattern with \texttt{\textbackslash boxed\{\}} answer formatting.
181	
182	\smallskip\noindent\textit{MMLU templates (representative MC format):}
183	\begin{enumerate}\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}\setlength{\parsep}{0pt}\setlength{\topsep}{0pt}
184	\item \texttt{The following are multiple choice questions (with answers) about \{subject\}.\textbackslash n\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash nA. ... D. ...\textbackslash nAnswer:}
185	\item \texttt{Answer the following \{subject\} questions by selecting the correct option.\textbackslash nQuestion: \{question\}\textbackslash n(A) ... (B) ... (C) ... (D) ...\textbackslash nCorrect answer:}
186	\item \texttt{Below are \{subject\} multiple-choice problems. Pick the right letter.\textbackslash nQ: \{question\}\textbackslash n~~A) ... B) ... C) ... D) ...\textbackslash nA:}
187	\item \texttt{Answer the following \{subject\} question by selecting A, B, C, or D.\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash n- A. ... - B. ... - C. ... - D. ...\textbackslash nAnswer:}
188	\item \texttt{Below is a \{subject\} exam question with four possible answers. Choose the correct one.\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash nOption A: ... Option B: ... Option C: ... Option D: ...\textbackslash nCorrect:}
189	\item \texttt{Question (\{subject\}):\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash nChoices: (A) ... (B) ... (C) ... (D) ...\textbackslash nYour answer:}
190	\end{enumerate}
191	
192	\smallskip\noindent\textit{GSM8K templates (representative FF format):}
193	\begin{enumerate}\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}\setlength{\parsep}{0pt}\setlength{\topsep}{0pt}
194	\item \texttt{Solve the following math problem step by step.\textbackslash nQ: \{question\}\textbackslash nA: Let's think step by step.}
195	\item \texttt{Answer the following math word problem. Show your work.\textbackslash nQuestion: \{question\}\textbackslash nSolution:}
196	\item \texttt{Solve each problem below. Write your reasoning, then give the final answer after \#\#\#\#.\textbackslash nProblem: \{question\}\textbackslash nReasoning:}
197	\item \texttt{You are a math tutor. Solve the problem and show your steps.\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash nSteps:}
198	\item \texttt{Work through this math question carefully.\textbackslash nQ: \{question\}\textbackslash nWork:}
199	\item \texttt{Math problem:\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash nLet's solve this step by step.}
200	\end{enumerate}
201	
202	\section{Use of AI Assistance}
203	We used Claude for coding, code drafting, debugging, and language polishing.
204

      "content": "\\section{Model and Prompt Details}\n\\label{app:models}\n\n\\paragraph{Models.} See Table~\\ref{tab:model_ids} (main text) for the eight models. Full HuggingFace identifiers: \\texttt{meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct}, \\texttt{google/gemma-2-9b-it}, \\texttt{mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3}, \\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, \\texttt{01-ai/Yi-1.5-9B-Chat}, \\texttt{allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct}, \\texttt{internlm/internlm2\\_5-7b-chat}, \\texttt{deepseek-ai/deepseek-llm-7b-chat}.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt templates.} Our six prompt templates follow a surface perturbation design inspired by BrittleBench \\cite{brittlebench2026}. For multiple-choice benchmarks, templates vary along three dimensions: option layout (lettered vs.\\ numbered), instruction wording (direct vs.\\ contextual), and answer prompt cue. For free-form benchmarks, templates vary chain-of-thought elicitation strategy. All six templates are semantically equivalent: they request the same task with different surface forms, to isolate formatting effects from content effects. ARC-Challenge and HellaSwag use the same six surface patterns as MMLU with domain-appropriate system instructions; MATH mirrors the GSM8K pattern with \\texttt{\\textbackslash boxed\\{\\}} answer formatting.\n\n\\smallskip\\noindent\\textit{MMLU templates (representative MC format):}\n\\begin{enumerate}\\setlength{\\itemsep}{0pt}\\setlength{\\parsep}{0pt}\\setlength{\\topsep}{0pt}\n\\item \\texttt{The following are multiple choice questions (with answers) about \\{subject\\}.\\textbackslash n\\textbackslash n\\{question\\}\\textbackslash nA. ... D. ...\\textbackslash nAnswer:}\n\\item \\texttt{Answer the following \\{subject\\} questions by selecting the correct option.\\textbackslash nQuestion: \\{question\\}\\textbackslash n(A) ... (B) ... (C) ... (D) ...\\textbackslash nCorrect answer:}\n\\item \\texttt{Below are \\{subject\\} multiple-choice problems. Pick the right letter.\\textbackslash nQ: \\{question\\}\\textbackslash n~~A) ... B) ... C) ... D) ...\\textbackslash nA:}\n\\item \\texttt{Answer the following \\{subject\\} question by selecting A, B, C, or D.\\textbackslash n\\{question\\}\\textbackslash n- A. ... - B. ... - C. ... - D. ...\\textbackslash nAnswer:}\n\\item \\texttt{Below is a \\{subject\\} exam question with four possible answers. Choose the correct one.\\textbackslash n\\{question\\}\\textbackslash nOption A: ... Option B: ... Option C: ... Option D: ...\\textbackslash nCorrect:}\n\\item \\texttt{Question (\\{subject\\}):\\textbackslash n\\{question\\}\\textbackslash nChoices: (A) ... (B) ... (C) ... (D) ...\\textbackslash nYour answer:}\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\smallskip\\noindent\\textit{GSM8K templates (representative FF format):}\n\\begin{enumerate}\\setlength{\\itemsep}{0pt}\\setlength{\\parsep}{0pt}\\setlength{\\topsep}{0pt}\n\\item \\texttt{Solve the following math problem step by step.\\textbackslash nQ: \\{question\\}\\textbackslash nA: Let's think step by step.}\n\\item \\texttt{Answer the following math word problem. Show your work.\\textbackslash nQuestion: \\{question\\}\\textbackslash nSolution:}\n\\item \\texttt{Solve each problem below. Write your reasoning, then give the final answer after \\#\\#\\#\\#.\\textbackslash nProblem: \\{question\\}\\textbackslash nReasoning:}\n\\item \\texttt{You are a math tutor. Solve the problem and show your steps.\\textbackslash n\\{question\\}\\textbackslash nSteps:}\n\\item \\texttt{Work through this math question carefully.\\textbackslash nQ: \\{question\\}\\textbackslash nWork:}\n\\item \\texttt{Math problem:\\textbackslash n\\{question\\}\\textbackslash nLet's solve this step by step.}\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\section{Use of AI Assistance}\nWe used Claude for coding, code drafting, debugging, and language polishing.\n",

_source line 2581 · 2026-05-26T10:45:27.538Z_

[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 🔍 论文自检：所有引用的逐项正确性检查（交叉验证）

请对论文里**每一条引用**做严格核对。AI 写作最致命的问题之一就是 hallucinate 引用——作者错、年份错、venue 错、标题对不上，甚至整篇论文压根不存在。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
检查范围
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

对 .bib 里的每一条 entry，以及正文里每一个 \cite{} 出现的位置：

□ 作者名拼写（中间名 / 缩写格式 / Unicode 字符）
□ 年份（很多 AI 把 NeurIPS 2022 paper 写成 2021）
□ 标题（前几个词最容易被 hallucinate 改写）
□ Venue / 会议名（NeurIPS vs NIPS / EMNLP vs EMNLP Findings / arXiv vs published version）
□ 卷期号 / 页码（journal 引用）
□ DOI / arXiv ID（如果有）
□ Paper 是否真实存在（hallucinated paper 是最严重的硬伤）
□ 正文里的 cite 用得对吗（cite 了 paper A 但说的是 paper B 的结论）

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
执行方式：Main Agent 派 2-3 个 Worker 做交叉验证
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

Main Agent 调用 spawn_worker 起 2-3 个独立 Worker，每个 Worker 独立完成全部引用的核对：

- 每个 Worker 拿到完整的 .bib 文件 + 正文 \cite 位置列表
- 用 Semantic Scholar API / Google Scholar / arXiv 搜索每一条
- 独立产出一份"引用核对清单"：每条标 ✓ 或 ✗ + 错误描述

Main Agent 收到所有 Worker 的清单后做交叉比对：

- ✅ 所有 Worker 都标 OK 的 → 接受
- ⚠️ Worker 之间结论不一致的 → Main Agent 亲自再核一遍
- ❌ 多数 Worker 标错的 → 必须修

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
修复策略
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

- 作者 / 年份 / 标题错 → 改成正确的
- venue 错（如 arxiv 版本和会议版本搞混）→ 改成应该引用的版本
- Paper 不存在（hallucinated）→ 删 cite + 重新找一个真实的同主题 paper 替代，如果找不到替代且这个 claim 离开 cite 就站不住，整段重写或删
- cite 用错位置（A 引到了 B）→ 改成正确的 cite key

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
汇报内容
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

(a) 总共多少条引用？
(b) 每个 Worker 各标出多少处问题？Worker 之间一致率？
(c) Main Agent 交叉比对后，最终确认有问题的引用数量
(d) 每一处的处理方式（修了 / 删了 / 替换了哪条新引用）
(e) 修完后 .bib 重新生成 + LaTeX 编译通过 + bibtex 没 warning

The user wants a thorough cross-validation of all citations in the paper. They want me to spawn multiple workers to independently verify every bib entry. Let me set this up properly.

First, let me count how many bib entries there are now. From what I've read, the references.bib has grown with the 13 new entries. Let me count:

Original entries (from the bib I read):
1. messing2026hidden
2. madaan2024quantifying
3. bouthillier2021accounting
4. shavelson1991generalizability
5. brennan2001generalizability
6. yuan2025layercast


7. messina2026background
8. thinkingmachines2025batch
9. shanmugavelu2024floating
10. brittlebench2026
11. sclar2024formatting
12. pezeshkpour2024ordering
13. hua2025flaw
14. bui2025seeds
15. polo2024tinybenchmarks
16. lalor2019irt
17. rodriguez2021leaderboards
18. perlitz2023efficient
19. zhou2026irt
20. kipnis2025metabench
21. henderson2018deep
22. dodge2020finetuning
23. dehghani2021benchmark
24. jiang2018bayesian
25. mush

So we're at 25 entries total, though the last one looks incomplete. I need to verify all of these across the workers.

Now I'm continuing through the bibliography with more foundational measurement theory papers—generalizability theory, item response theory, differential item functioning, and bootstrap methods—alongside recent work on evaluation frameworks and model benchmarks. The new entries add major language models from the past couple years and key benchmark datasets like MMLU and ARC.

I've got 50 total entries now. The user wants me to set up parallel verification across all of them using multiple workers who'll independently check each citation against Semantic Scholar, Google Scholar, and arXiv. Even though the original 37 were already verified in earlier rounds and the 13 new ones were checked against arXiv, they're asking for a fresh comprehensive pass to ensure everything is accurate.

I'll create 3 agents to work in parallel, each independently validating the full bibliography. Each agent will read the references file, systematically verify the title, authors, year, and venue for every entry through web searches, confirm the citations are used appropriately in context, and produce a verification checklist.

### Tool result

_source line 2613 · 2026-05-26T10:53:05.439Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Had6VS98ghpLaX28KvZ4YM`</summary>

```text
1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2	
```

{
  "text": "最新 abstract 全文：\n\n---\n\nLLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34–38% in multiple-choice), and the model×item interaction is the second largest (30–36% in MC, 15–17% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude—models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt × 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 (G ≥ 0.80). We observe a format-associated pattern (n = 5, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68–72%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31–41%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit.\n\n---\n\n~199 words，符合 ARR ≤200 words 限制。"
}

[飞书用户 孙震 (ou_12185aa391efc8ff13eea9c848f0ab5a)]: Keywords: Generalizability theory, LLM evaluation, variance decomposition, reproducibility, benchmark design, evaluation methodology, D-study, multiple-choice, free-form generation, item analysis
Abstract:
LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34–38% in multiple-choice), and the model×item interaction is the second largest (30–36% in MC, 15–17% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude—models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt × 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 (G ≥ 0.80). We observe a format-associated pattern (n = 5, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68–72%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31–41%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit.

For the D-study projections claim, Table 7 contains the G-coefficient values and item count thresholds, while Table 8 has the cross-model budgets with confidence intervals, so "Tables 7–8" appears correct there.

One more issue: the elaboration states "|ΔG| = 0.035" for the four-model subset stability check, but the actual text in §4.5 reports "|ΔG| ≤ 0.029 for MC, ≤ 0.065 for FF"—the 0.035 is the mean from the table, not the stated bound.

Alright, I think I've identified the main discrepancies. Time to compile my findings.
```

[飞书用户 孙震 (ou_12185aa391efc8ff13eea9c848f0ab5a)]: research area keywords需要从这里面选

Area Keywords at ARR
Choosing the right track may be tricky for the authors, and to aid them, in this page we provide the keywords associated with ARR tracks.
This list was initially based on ACL’23 list. In April 2024 we added Language Modeling and Human-Centered NLP tracks, in May 2025 the keywords were updated based on input from EMNLP’25 PCs. Starting from March 2026 the following tracks will be used, based on input from ACL’26 and EMNLP’26 program chairs. This list is overall expected to change over time. It is not meant to be exhaustive, but it should still give some idea of what is the focus of different areas. In the future, they can be used to analyze the submission volume for different subtopics (so that we could tell which areas are growing and may need their own areas), and for paper-reviewer matching.
How does one choose the right area, given that there are so many overlaps? Here are a few examples:
- Should a question answering resource paper go to the resource area or the question answering area? Generally, if there is a task-specific area, it takes both modeling and resource papers, but if your paper is very centered on data collection methodology, you might still want to consider the resource area.
- What about a paper on multilingual generation? It could go for the generation area if the focus is on generation strategies, or to the multilingualism area - if the focus is on those languages.
- What about work in low-resource settings - is it efficiency or multilingualism? If the focus is on the training, and the low-resource setting is simulated from a well-resourced language, then we’d suggest the efficiency area. If you are contributing resources, analysis, or new solutions for languages that haven’t had that kind of thing before, consider the multilingualism area.
The current ARR areas are as follows.
- Clinical and Biomedical Applications: biomedical knowledge extraction, discovery, and text mining; biomedical question answering; clinical and biomedical language models; clinical dialogue systems; clinical and biomedical text summarisation; NLP tools for biomedical knowledge graphs, clinical decision support, clinical coding, drug adverse event discovery, mental health; phrase mining and disease association; regulatory and ethical considerations; other clinical and biomedical applications which explicitly involve natural language as a central element in the paper contribution.
- Computational Social Science and Cultural Analytics: human behavior analysis; stance detection; frame detection and analysis; hate-speech detection; misinformation detection and analysis; psycho-demographic trait prediction; emotion detection and analysis; emoji prediction and analysis; language/cultural bias analysis; sociolinguistics; NLP tools for social analysis; quantitative analyses of news and/or social media;
- Dialogue and Interactive Systems: spoken dialogue systems; evaluation and metrics; task-oriented; bias/toxicity; factuality; retrieval; knowledge augmented; commonsense reasoning; interactive storytelling; embodied agents; applications; multi-modal dialogue systems; grounded dialog; multilingual / low resource; dialogue state tracking; conversational modeling;
- Discourse and Pragmatics: anaphora resolution; coreference resolution; bridging resolution; coherence; cohesion; discourse relations; discourse parsing; dialogue; conversation; discourse and multilinguality; argument mining; communication; discourse-level inference; inter-sentential reasoning; pragmatic inference and reasoning.
- Efficient Methods for NLP: quantization; pruning; distillation; parameter-efficient-training; data-efficient training; data augmentation; LLM efficiency; NLP in resource-constrained settings;
- Ethics, Bias, and Fairness: data ethics; model alignment; model bias/fairness evaluation; model bias/unfairness mitigation; ethical considerations in NLP applications; transparency; policy and governance; reflections and critiques;
- Generation: human evaluation; automatic evaluation; multilingualism; efficient models; few-shot generation; analysis; domain adaptation; data-to-text generation; text-to-text generation; inference methods; model architectures; retrieval-augmented generation; interactive and collaborative generation;
- Human-Centered NLP and Human-AI Interaction: human-AI interaction/cooperation; human-in-the-loop; human-centered evaluation; user-centered design; value-centered design; human factors in NLP; participatory/community-based NLP; values and culture;
- Information Extraction: named entity recognition and relation extraction; event extraction; open information extraction; knowledge base construction; entity linking/disambiguation; document-level extraction; multilingual extraction; zero/few-shot extraction;
- Information Retrieval and Text Mining: passage retrieval; dense retrieval; document representation; hashing; re-ranking; pre-training; contrastive learning; retrieval for RAG;
- Interpretability and Analysis of Models for NLP: adversarial attacks/examples/training; calibration/uncertainty; counterfactual/contrastive explanations; data influence; data shortcuts/artifacts; explanation faithfulness; feature attribution; free-text/natural language explanations; hardness of samples; hierarchical & concept explanations; human-subject application-grounded evaluations; knowledge tracing/discovering/inducing; model editing; probing; robustness; topic modeling;
- Language Modeling: adversarial attacks; chain-of-thought; fine-tuning; continual learning; hallucinations; neurosymbolic approaches; pre-training; privacy; prompting; safety; scaling; security; sparse models; red teaming; retrieval-augmented language models; robustness; transfer; watermarking;
- LLM agents: tool use; function calling; multi-modal agents; multi-agent systems; planning in agents; agent communication; agent coordination and negotiation; environment interaction; agent memory; reinforcement learning in agents; LLM-based controllers; agent evaluation; grounded agents; embodied agents;
- Linguistic Theories, Cognitive Modeling, and Psycholinguistics: linguistic theories; cognitive modeling; computational psycholinguistics;
- Machine Learning for NLP: graph-based methods; knowledge-augmented methods; multi-task learning; self-supervised learning; contrastive learning; generative models; data augmentation; word embeddings; structured prediction; transfer learning / domain adaptation; representation learning; generalization; few-shot learning; reinforcement learning; optimization methods; continual learning; adversarial training; meta learning; causality; graphical models; human-in-the-loop / active learning;
- Machine Translation: automatic evaluation; biases; domain adaptation; efficient inference for MT; efficient MT training; few-shot/zero-shot MT; human evaluation; interactive MT; MT deployment and maintenance; MT theory; modeling; multilingual MT; multimodality; online adaptation for MT; parallel decoding/non-autoregressive MT; pre-training for MT; scaling; speech translation; switch-code translation; vocabulary learning;
- Multilingualism and Cross-Lingual NLP: code-switching; mixed language; multilingualism; language contact; language change; linguistic variation; cross-lingual transfer; multilingual representations; multilingual pre-training; multilingual benchmarks; multilingual evaluation; dialects and language varieties; less-resourced languages; endangered languages; indigenous languages; minoritized languages; language documentation; resources for less-resourced languages; software and tools;

- NLP and Code Models: language-to-code generation, code-to-language generation, other topics which explicitly involve natural language as a central element in the paper contribution.
- NLP and Symbolic Reasoning: natural language AND symbolic reasoning topics (e.g. mathematical / symbolic

_source line 2652 · 2026-05-26T12:02:20.487Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U41e3LuANW9F6MRsDUQ9VC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Item difficulty is the largest variance component in LLM evaluation (34--38\% in MC), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in FF), exceeding all manipulated design factors by an order of magnitude. This reveals that models disagree on which items are difficult rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. Multi-facet evaluation designs absorb this interaction: a recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1$\times$ reduction), with cross-benchmark budgets of 93--128 items. An exploratory format comparison ($n = 5$ benchmarks) suggests MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\%, motivating per-format budget calibration pending broader confirmation. We recommend three practices: adopt multi-facet designs for published results, report item-level accuracy to expose model$\times$item interaction, and calibrate item budgets per benchmark format.
5	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KdpQb88EeAAh6CCXvrYkQx`</summary>

````text
1	# Generalizability Theory for LLM Evaluation Variance Decomposition
2	
3	Code and data for applying Generalizability Theory (G-theory) to decompose variance in LLM benchmark evaluations, using Henderson Method I to estimate variance components across evaluation facets (prompt template, temperature, random seed, item ordering).
4	
5	## Requirements
6	
7	- Python 3.10+
8	- GPU with CUDA support (for vLLM inference)
9	
10	```bash
11	pip install -r requirements.txt
12	```
13	
14	## Quick Start
15	
16	**1. Prepare data and design matrix**
17	
18	```bash
19	python -m src.data.sample_mmlu --output data/mmlu_items.json
20	python -m src.data.few_shot --output data/few_shot_examples.json
21	python -m src.design.generate_design --output design_matrix.csv
22	```
23	
24	**2. Run inference**
25	
26	```bash
27	python -m src.inference.run_experiment \
28	    --design design_matrix.csv \
29	    --items data/mmlu_items.json \
30	    --few-shot data/few_shot_examples.json \
31	    --output results/all_results.jsonl \
32	    --gpu-id 0
33	```
34	
35	**3. G-study: variance decomposition**
36	
37	```bash
38	python -m src.analysis.variance_decomposition \
39	    --input results/all_results.jsonl \
40	    --output results/variance_components.json \
41	    --n-bootstrap 1000
42	```
43	
44	**4. D-study: budget optimization**
45	
46	```bash
47	python -m src.analysis.d_study \
48	    --input results/variance_components.json \
49	    --output results/d_study_results.json
50	```
51	
52	The D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G >= 0.90, 0.95, 0.99).
53	
54	## Repository Structure
55	
56	```
57	src/
58	  data/           Data loading and sampling
59	  design/         Experimental design matrix generation
60	  inference/      vLLM-based model inference
61	  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization
62	scripts/          Experiment scripts for reproducing paper results
63	data/             Benchmark items and few-shot examples (JSON)
64	paper/            LaTeX source
65	tests/            Unit tests
66	```
67	
68	## License
69	
70	MIT
71	
````

</details>

D-study: budget optimization**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.d_study \\\n    --input results/variance_components.json \\\n    --output results/d_study_results.json\n```\n\nThe D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G >= 0.90, 0.95, 0.99).\n\n## Repository Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n  data/           Data loading and sampling\n  design/         Experimental design matrix generation\n  inference/      vLLM-based model inference\n  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization\nscripts/          Experiment scripts for reproducing paper results\ndata/             Benchmark items and few-shot examples (JSON)\npaper/            LaTeX source\ntests/            Unit tests\n```\n\n## License\n\nMIT\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XmFHbm8E9N8NgHr3vvvQVP`</summary>

```text
1c1
< Standard LLM benchmarks evaluate each model under a single prompt template and random seed, implicitly treating these design choices as negligible variance sources. We show this assumption is wrong and that it inflates item requirements by an order of magnitude. Applying Generalizability theory---a psychometric framework for multi-facet reliability analysis---to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3 million binary records), we find that the dominant evaluation variance source is not the prompt, the seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), meaning models disagree on which specific items are difficult rather than on overall ability. Multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this interaction noise, achieving reliable measurement with roughly 100 items where single-condition protocols require over 1,000. We further discover that the variance structure is format-dependent: item-related factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. These findings establish design-dependent reproducibility budgets for LLM evaluation and suggest that reliability standards should be calibrated per benchmark format. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
---
> LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
```

        "content": "# Generalizability Theory for LLM Evaluation Variance Decomposition\n\nCode and data for applying Generalizability Theory (G-theory) to decompose variance in LLM benchmark evaluations, using Henderson Method I to estimate variance components across evaluation facets (prompt template, temperature, random seed, item ordering).\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python 3.10+\n- GPU with CUDA support (for vLLM inference)\n\n```bash\npip install -r requirements.txt\n```\n\n## Quick Start\n\n**1. Prepare data and design matrix**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.data.sample_mmlu --output data/mmlu_items.json\npython -m src.data.few_shot --output data/few_shot_examples.json\npython -m src.design.generate_design --output design_matrix.csv\n```\n\n**2. Run inference**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.inference.run_experiment \\\n    --design design_matrix.csv \\\n    --items data/mmlu_items.json \\\n    --few-shot data/few_shot_examples.json \\\n    --output results/all_results.jsonl \\\n    --gpu-id 0\n```\n\n**3. G-study: variance decomposition**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.variance_decomposition \\\n    --input results/all_results.jsonl \\\n    --output results/variance_components.json \\\n    --n-bootstrap 1000\n```\n\n**4. D-study: budget optimization**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.d_study \\\n    --input results/variance_components.json \\\n    --output results/d_study_results.json\n```\n\nThe D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G >= 0.90, 0.95, 0.99).\n\n## Repository Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n  data/           Data loading and sampling\n  design/         Experimental design matrix generation\n  inference/      vLLM-based model inference\n  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization\nscripts/          Experiment scripts for reproducing paper results\ndata/             Benchmark items and few-shot examples (JSON)\npaper/            LaTeX source\ntests/            Unit tests\n```\n\n## License\n\nMIT\n",

        "totalLines": 71

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "a346b47d-47ff-408c-8857-eecfee5e38dc",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\\% in MC, 15--17\\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \\geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.\n",
        "numLines": 2,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 2
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "1c877f11-5eed-46e4-a934-984a49524d93",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method and Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Generalizability Theory}\n\\label{sec:gtheory}\n\nGeneralizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \\cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \\emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.\n\nA \\emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \\cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals \\cite{efron1993bootstrap} (item-level resampling with replacement, preserving the fully-crossed structure across all other facets) \\cite{li2023bootstrap}.\n\nA \\emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:\n\\begin{equation}\nG_\\text{item} = \\frac{\\sigma^2_p}{\\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_\\text{rel}}\n\\label{eq:gcoeff}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma^2_\\text{rel} = \\sum_i \\sigma^2_{p \\times f_i} / n_{f_i} + \\sigma^2_\\text{residual} / \\prod_i n_{f_i}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \\cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. A D-study projects reliability for a test averaging over $n_p$ items via\n\\begin{equation}\nG(n_p) = \\frac{n_p \\, \\sigma^2_p}{n_p \\, \\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_\\text{rel}}\n\\label{eq:dstudy}\n\\end{equation}\nenabling independent verification of all budget projections in Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy}. This prospective capability distinguishes G-theory from classical ANOVA: rather than only attributing variance post hoc, a D-study enables optimization of evaluation designs \\emph{before} data collection \\cite{shavelson1989gtheory}. Figure~\\ref{fig:dstudy_curves} illustrates the practical impact: multi-facet designs achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ with 55 items versus 552 under single-condition protocols. We define \\emph{item-related variance} as the sum of the item main effect ($\\sigma^2_p$) and the model$\\times$item interaction ($\\sigma^2_{pM}$). The latter captures differential item functioning (DIF): items whose difficulty ranking changes across models \\cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. Because DIF is operationally an item-level property (it reflects model-specific item difficulty profiles rather than a global model ability shift); aggregating $\\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_{pM}$ provides a unified measure of item-driven variance. We report unbundled components throughout to ensure transparency.\\footnote{Notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\\pi$ = precision, $\\delta$ = prompt, $s$ = seed, $\\tau$ = temperature, $o$ = ordering, $\\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$.}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_dstudy_curves}\n\\caption{D-study projection: the full multi-facet design (blue, 1,296 conditions; theoretical upper bound, impractical for routine use) reaches $G \\geq 0.80$ at 55 items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (red). The recommended 3$\\times$3 design achieves 178 items (3.1$\\times$ reduction).}\n\\label{fig:dstudy_curves}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design: MMLU}\n\\label{sec:exp001}\n\nWe design a fully crossed factorial experiment (exp-001) that simultaneously varies seven facets of LLM evaluation on MMLU. Table~\\ref{tab:design_matrix} summarizes the complete design matrix.\n\n\\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions via stratified sampling across 10 subjects (20 per subject, four domains: STEM, humanities, social sciences, professional; coefficient of variation [CV] = 3.1\\%). Models ($M$): eight open-weight 7--9B instruction-tuned models (Table~\\ref{tab:model_ids}), treated as a quasi-random facet (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}). Numerical precision ($\\pi$): BFloat16 (BF16), Float16 (FP16), Float32 (FP32). Prompt template ($\\delta$): six surface perturbations \\cite{sclar2024formatting} varying layout and wording (Appendix~\\ref{app:models}). Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 256, 512, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\\tau$): 0.0, 0.3, 0.7. Answer ordering ($o$): four permutations of few-shot demonstrations \\cite{pezeshkpour2024ordering}.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample sizes.} The cross-model BF16 balanced subset yields 460,800 records (8 models $\\times$ 200 items $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 2 temperatures $\\times$ 4 orderings). A single-model analysis (Llama) spans all three precisions, yielding 259,200 binary observations across 1,296 fully balanced conditions. The BF16 subset serves as the primary cross-model sample because BF16 is the standard inference precision for these architectures.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Fully crossed G-theory design. Exp-001 evaluates MMLU across all facet levels; exp-002 extends to five benchmarks with reduced crossing. $^\\dagger$BF16 balanced subset (2 temperatures: 0.0, 0.7) for cross-model analysis; the full single-model design uses all 3 levels.}\n\\label{tab:design_matrix}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nFacet & Levels & exp-001 & exp-002 \\\\\n\\midrule\nItem ($p$) & Stratified sample & 200 & 200$\\times$5 \\\\\nModel ($M$) & 8 architecture families & 8 & 8 \\\\\nPrecision ($\\pi$) & BF16 / FP16 / FP32 & 3 & 1 \\\\\nPrompt ($\\delta$) & Paraphrases & 6 & 6 \\\\\nSeed ($s$) & Fixed set & 6 & 6 \\\\\nTemperature ($\\tau$) & 0.0 / 0.3 / 0.7 & 3 & 2 \\\\\nOrdering ($o$) & Demo permutations & 4 & 4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{Total records} & 460,800$^\\dagger$ & 2,304,000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Extension}\n\\label{sec:exp002}\n\nExp-002 extends to five benchmarks: MMLU \\cite{hendrycks2021mmlu}, ARC-Challenge \\cite{clark2018arc}, HellaSwag \\cite{zellers2019hellaswag} (MC), GSM8K \\cite{cobbe2021gsm8k}, and MATH \\cite{hendrycks2021math} (FF). The design crosses 8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items, totaling 2,304,000 records. This reduced crossing fixes precision at BF16 to concentrate power on format differences. Ordering operates only on MMLU ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ for other benchmarks, retained for design balance). Subset analysis confirms this preserves the full design's variance structure ($\\Delta G = 0.0008$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:protocol}\n\nAll evaluations use generation-based scoring (free-text response with answer extraction) rather than log-likelihood ranking, following \\citet{hua2025flaw}. Binary correctness (1/0) is the primary metric; text-exact-match is secondary. Inference uses vLLM~0.9.2 without batch-invariant kernels, preserving CUDA-level nondeterminism as a measurable variance source \\cite{messina2026background, thinkingmachines2025batch}. Few-shot protocols: 5-shot MMLU/ARC, 4-shot MATH, 8-shot GSM8K, 10-shot HellaSwag.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Data Assumptions}\n\\label{sec:binary}\n\nStandard G-theory assumes continuous data \\cite{shavelson1991generalizability}; our binary scores violate this. Henderson Method~I produces unbiased estimates for balanced designs regardless of distribution \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}, and restricted maximum likelihood (REML) differs by $<$0.5pp. A probit simulation, generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) complement ($\\rho = 1.0$), and accuracy-range analysis confirm robustness (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\n\nAll findings in this paper are conditional on binary correctness scoring. Changing the scoring metric can fundamentally reshape variance structure: text-exact-match scoring collapses $G_\\text{item}$ from 0.936 to 0.007 (\\S\\ref{sec:single_model}), and partial-credit scoring on GSM8K reduces $G_\\text{item}$ from 0.86 to 0.77 (mean $\\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). Readers should not assume that the variance decomposition or D-study budgets reported here transfer to non-binary rubrics.\n\n\\subsection{Model Facet Specification}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Models used in all experiments. RMS = RMSNorm, LN = LayerNorm.}\n\\label{tab:model_ids}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\columnwidth}{@{\\extracolsep{\\fill}}llrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nShort name & Family & Params & Norm \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & Llama-3 & 8B & RMS \\\\\nGemma-2-9B & Gemma-2 & 9B & RMS \\\\\nMistral-7B-v0.3 & Mistral & 7B & RMS \\\\\nQwen3-8B & Qwen-3 & 8B & RMS \\\\\nYi-1.5-9B & Yi-1.5 & 9B & RMS \\\\\nOLMo-2-7B & OLMo-2 & 7B & RMS \\\\\nInternLM2.5-7B & InternLM-2 & 7B & RMS \\\\\nDeepSeek-7B & DeepSeek & 7B & RMS \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\end{table}\n\nOur target universe comprises open-weight, instruction-tuned models in the 7--9B parameter range (Table~\\ref{tab:model_ids}); results may not generalize to API-based, base, or $>$9B models. The eight models are treated as a quasi-random facet \\cite{meredith1993measurement, putnick2016measurement}. We report $G$ under random-model assumptions, the more conservative specification: treating models as fixed yields $G_\\text{fixed}$ 0.951--0.990 vs.\\ $G_\\text{random}$ 0.863--0.896 (increase of 0.077--0.113; Table~\\ref{tab:fixed_vs_random}), because model$\\times$item interaction (15--36\\% across benchmarks) enters relative error variance. Consequently, D-study budgets are upper bounds. Leave-one-model-out (LOMO) analysis preserves component rankings across all five benchmarks (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}); four-model subset checks match within $|\\Delta G| = 0.035$ (\\S\\ref{sec:dstudy_budgets}).\n\\label{sec:model_facet}\n",
        "numLines": 106,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 106
      }
    },

_source line 2757 · 2026-05-26T12:27:14.680Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EAxAq8A9UkvbHSQVTcTKAN`</summary>

````text
1	# Generalizability Theory for LLM Evaluation Variance Decomposition
2	
3	Code and data for applying Generalizability Theory (G-theory) to decompose variance in LLM benchmark evaluations, using Henderson Method I to estimate variance components across evaluation facets (prompt template, temperature, random seed, item ordering).
4	
5	## Requirements
6	
7	- Python 3.10+
8	- GPU with CUDA support (for vLLM inference)
9	
10	```bash
11	pip install -r requirements.txt
12	```
13	
14	## Quick Start
15	
16	**1. Prepare data and design matrix**
17	
18	```bash
19	python -m src.data.sample_mmlu --output data/mmlu_items.json
20	python -m src.data.few_shot --output data/few_shot_examples.json
21	python -m src.design.generate_design --output design_matrix.csv
22	```
23	
24	**2. Run inference**
25	
26	```bash
27	python -m src.inference.run_experiment \
28	    --design design_matrix.csv \
29	    --items data/mmlu_items.json \
30	    --few-shot data/few_shot_examples.json \
31	    --output results/all_results.jsonl \
32	    --gpu-id 0
33	```
34	
35	**3. G-study: variance decomposition**
36	
37	```bash
38	python -m src.analysis.variance_decomposition \
39	    --input results/all_results.jsonl \
40	    --output results/variance_components.json \
41	    --n-bootstrap 1000
42	```
43	
44	**4. D-study: budget optimization**
45	
46	```bash
47	python -m src.analysis.d_study \
48	    --input results/variance_components.json \
49	    --output results/d_study_results.json
50	```
51	
52	The D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G >= 0.90, 0.95, 0.99).
53	
54	## Repository Structure
55	
56	```
57	src/
58	  data/           Data loading and sampling
59	  design/         Experimental design matrix generation
60	  inference/      vLLM-based model inference
61	  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization
62	scripts/          Experiment scripts for reproducing paper results
63	data/             Benchmark items and few-shot examples (JSON)
64	paper/            LaTeX source
65	tests/            Unit tests
66	```
67	
68	## License
69	
70	MIT
71	
````

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019gqpmWHQzZxu2xMLjdmT1L`</summary>

```text
1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2	
```

````json
{
  "file_path": "/tmp/nlp_vg_sync/README.md",
  "content": "# Reproducibility Budgets for Open-Weight LLM Evaluation: A Generalizability Theory Study of 7–9B Instruction-Tuned Models\n\nCode and data for applying Generalizability Theory (G-theory) to decompose variance in LLM benchmark evaluations. We use Henderson Method I to estimate variance components across seven evaluation facets (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, sampling temperature, answer ordering, model, item) in a factorial experiment crossing eight open-weight 7–9B instruction-tuned models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, MATH; 2.3M records).\n\n**Key findings:**\n- Item difficulty is the largest variance component (34–38% in multiple-choice)\n- Model×item interaction is the second largest (30–36% in MC, 15–17% in free-form), exceeding all manipulated design factors by an order of magnitude\n- A 3-prompt × 3-seed protocol achieves G ≥ 0.80 with 178 items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1× reduction)\n- Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68–72%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31–41%)\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python 3.10+\n- GPU with CUDA support (for vLLM inference)\n\n```bash\npip install -r requirements.txt\n```\n\n## Quick Start\n\n**1. Prepare data and design matrix**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.data.sample_mmlu --output data/mmlu_items.json\npython -m src.data.few_shot --output data/few_shot_examples.json\npython -m src.design.generate_design --output design_matrix.csv\n```\n\n**2. Run inference**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.inference.run_experiment \\\n    --design design_matrix.csv \\\n    --items data/mmlu_items.json \\\n    --few-shot data/few_shot_examples.json \\\n    --output results/all_results.jsonl \\\n    --gpu-id 0\n```\n\n**3. G-study: variance decomposition**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.variance_decomposition \\\n    --input results/all_results.jsonl \\\n    --output results/variance_components.json \\\n    --n-bootstrap 1000\n```\n\n**4. D-study: budget optimization**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.d_study \\\n    --input results/variance_components.json \\\n    --output results/d_study_results.json\n```\n\nThe D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G ≥ 0.80, 0.90, 0.95).\n\n## Repository Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n  data/           Data loading and sampling\n  design/         Experimental design matrix generation\n  inference/      vLLM-based model inference\n  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization\nscripts/          Experiment scripts for reproducing paper results\ndata/             Benchmark items and few-shot examples (JSON)\npaper/            LaTeX source\ntests/            Unit tests\n```\n\n## License\n\nMIT\n"
}
````

    "originalFile": "# Generalizability Theory for LLM Evaluation Variance Decomposition\n\nCode and data for applying Generalizability Theory (G-theory) to decompose variance in LLM benchmark evaluations, using Henderson Method I to estimate variance components across evaluation facets (prompt template, temperature, random seed, item ordering).\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python 3.10+\n- GPU with CUDA support (for vLLM inference)\n\n```bash\npip install -r requirements.txt\n```\n\n## Quick Start\n\n**1. Prepare data and design matrix**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.data.sample_mmlu --output data/mmlu_items.json\npython -m src.data.few_shot --output data/few_shot_examples.json\npython -m src.design.generate_design --output design_matrix.csv\n```\n\n**2. Run inference**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.inference.run_experiment \\\n    --design design_matrix.csv \\\n    --items data/mmlu_items.json \\\n    --few-shot data/few_shot_examples.json \\\n    --output results/all_results.jsonl \\\n    --gpu-id 0\n```\n\n**3. G-study: variance decomposition**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.variance_decomposition \\\n    --input results/all_results.jsonl \\\n    --output results/variance_components.json \\\n    --n-bootstrap 1000\n```\n\n**4. D-study: budget optimization**\n\n```bash\npython -m src.analysis.d_study \\\n    --input results/variance_components.json \\\n    --output results/d_study_results.json\n```\n\nThe D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G >= 0.90, 0.95, 0.99).\n\n## Repository Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n  data/           Data loading and sampling\n  design/         Experimental design matrix generation\n  inference/      vLLM-based model inference\n  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization\nscripts/          Experiment scripts for reproducing paper results\ndata/             Benchmark items and few-shot examples (JSON)\npaper/            LaTeX source\ntests/            Unit tests\n```\n\n## License\n\nMIT\n",
    "userModified": false
  },
  "sourceToolAssistantUUID": "f3c971d8-20b7-4e9a-975b-4d4f0e2c3984",
  "userType": "external",

}
